# HANDBOOK
## OF PHYSICS
### RESTORED.

->In which the true harmony of Nature is explained, and many errors of ancient <term>Philosophy</term> <note>In this context, "Philosophy" refers to Natural Philosophy—the study of the physical world and its laws before the modern definition of "science" existed.</note> are clearly revealed through rules and certain demonstrations.<-

->*A second treatise entitled:*<-

## THE SECRET WORK
### OF HERMETIC PHILOSOPHY. <note>Hermetic Philosophy refers to a tradition of knowledge associated with the legendary figure Hermes Trismegistus, encompassing alchemy, astrology, and spiritual transformation.</note>

->In which the secrets of Nature and Art concerning the matter of the <term>Philosophers' Stone</term> <note>original: "Lapidis Philosophorum"; the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and granting eternal life, often used as a metaphor for spiritual perfection.</note> and the method of working are made manifest according to rule and in an orderly way.<-

->*Both works by the same anonymous Author.*<- <note>Though published anonymously, the author is known to be Jean d'Espagnet (1564–1637), a French lawyer and politician with a deep interest in alchemy.</note>

->MY HOPE IS IN THE LAMB.<- <note>original: "SPES MEA EST IN AGNO"; a religious motto likely referring to the "Lamb of God" (Christ), often used by d'Espagnet as a personal device.</note>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page contains an introductory poem (epigram) praising the work "Physics Restored" by Jean d'Espagnet. It follows the title page translated previously and sets a celebratory tone for the "restoration" of natural science from the obscurity of the Middle Ages.</meta>

# ON PHYSICS
# RESTORED.
## *EPIGRAM.*

> Behold the rising radiance of Physics, which by an ill-fated hand
> dark Barbarism had submerged in the waters of the Styx <note>original: "Stygiis... vadis"; the Styx is the river of the underworld in Greek mythology, representing death or the "darkness" of lost knowledge.</note>.
> Thus Physics sees the damages to her beauty and her dowry repaid
> so that she hopes to attract even more suitors.
> O amazement! O, whom does she not strike with wondrous love!
> Venus herself did not rise more gracefully from her father’s sea.
> This golden one <note>A personification of the science of Physics.</note> does not think herself merely restored by these writings,
> but born anew from the laboring brain of Jove <note>original: "Iouis"; a reference to the myth of Athena, the goddess of wisdom, who was born fully grown from the forehead of Jupiter (Jove).</note>.

<insert># 0-54</insert> <meta>Handwritten shelf mark</meta>

->GAIUS.<- <note>The Latin name of the person who likely composed this introductory poem in honor of the author.</note>

<note>Vocabulary: **Physics** (Study of nature), **Epigram** (A short, witty poem), **Gaius** (The poem's author), **Venus** (Goddess of love/beauty), **Jove** (Jupiter, king of the gods), **Stygian** (Relating to the underworld/darkness).</note>

<summary>A short, laudatory poem celebrating the "rebirth" of Physics. It uses classical mythology to suggest that the book rescues natural science from medieval "barbarism" and presents it as a pure, divine form of wisdom.</summary>
<keywords>Physics, Epigram, Latin Poetry, Mythology, Restoration of Knowledge, Jean d'Espagnet</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the celebratory introductory poem by Gaius to the author’s own preface. The previous page established a theme of "restoring" Physics from darkness; here, the author, Jean d'Espagnet, begins his address to the reader by explaining his retirement from political life to pursue natural philosophy.</meta>

<image-desc>A horizontal row of decorative floral fleurons serves as a header ornament.</image-desc>

# TO THE STUDENTS
## OF THE LIGHT OF NATURE, GREETINGS. <note>original: "S." abbreviating "Salutem," a standard Latin greeting.</note>

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'P' containing floral and leaf motifs.</image-desc>

**P**ost-retirement, having recently withdrawn myself by my own choice from public cares and the stormy sandbanks <note>original: "syrtibus"; a reference to the Syrtes, dangerous quicksands off the coast of North Africa, used here as a metaphor for the perils of political life.</note> of the Court into the peaceful shallows of domestic tranquility, (O students of the **Secrets of Nature** <note>original: "Arcanorum Naturae"</note>) that saying of the poet <note>The Roman poet Ovid, from his work *Amores* (3.11.3).</note> frequently occurred to my exulting mind:

> *Truly now I have claimed myself and fled my chains;* <note>original: "Scilicet asserui iam me fugique cathenas"</note>

I felt that old contemplation of the things of Nature returning, which had once grown with me from my early youth; and I willingly offered myself to it as it opportunely arrived, so that the splendor of **Fortune** <note>Success, wealth, or status in the public eye.</note> which I had voluntarily renounced might be compensated by a certain present benefit of its own light, Na- <note>The word is cut off at the end of the page; it likely continues as "Natura" (Nature) on the following leaf.</note>

<vocab>Lucis Physicae, Arcanorum Naturae, Fortunae, cathenas</vocab>

<summary>In this preface, the author describes his retirement from the "stormy" life of the royal court to the peace of his home. He explains that this solitude allows him to return to his lifelong passion: the study of the secrets of nature.</summary>

<keywords>Preface, Retirement, Secrets of Nature, Natural Philosophy, Ovid, Fortune, Light of Nature</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's preface to the reader. The previous page ended mid-word with "Na-", which completes here as "Naturae" (Nature). The author is justifying his transition from a high-stakes legal and political career to the study of natural philosophy.</meta>

[Na]ture’s care might repay me; and also that I might vindicate myself from public accusation. For an indictment for deserting the laws of my country and my public duties already seemed prepared against me, as if I were a runaway <note>original: "transfugam"; the author uses the language of military desertion to describe his retirement from public office.</note>. Lest they should use their votes to condemn me as an outlaw under such a title, I fled to the sacred protection of the secret laws of the **Universe** <note>original: "Vniversi"</note>—to which the name of our "common homeland" is truly owed—which might safeguard my innocence from slander. 

For a sense of decency will not allow political laws to decree a harsh sentence against a man who, having set aside the anxieties of civil cares, has devoted himself to the study and cultivation of the **Worldly Republic** <note>original: "mundanæ Reipublicæ"; d’Espagnet is invoking the Stoic concept of the *Cosmopolis*, suggesting that the entire natural world is a greater state than any single kingdom.</note>. 

Therefore, as I turned my mind toward the empire of **Nature**, its laws, order, governance, harmony <note>original: "concentum"; suggesting a musical or mathematical concord in the way the world functions.</note>, effects, causes, and finally its immense riches, I was first seized by **wonder** <note>original: "admiratio"</note>. For noble minds, wonder is no less a spur to acquiring knowledge than it is an admission of ignorance; for wonder [drives] the mind into 

<summary>Jean d'Espagnet justifies his retirement from political life by arguing that studying the "Worldly Republic" of Nature is a higher form of citizenship. He introduces "wonder" as the essential starting point for scientific discovery and the pursuit of natural laws.</summary>

<keywords>natural philosophy, civic duty, Cosmopolis, laws of nature, wonder, scientific inquiry, retirement</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's preface. The previous page ended with "wonder [drives] the mind into", which is completed here by "sublimē rapit" (snatches it upward).</meta>

...it snatches [the mind] upward; by this movement, one is inflamed with a desire to acquire knowledge of a thing which it is a shame to ignore. The mind, warming up through such exercise, by bringing back the many decrees of ancient and primary **Philosophy** <note>original: "Principísque Philosophiæ"; likely referring to the Peripatetic or Aristotelian school which was the "primary" authority of the time.</note> to the anvil <note>original: "sub incudem reuocando"; a Latin idiom meaning to critically re-examine, polish, or rework a piece of thought.</note>, refused to assent to them. For **Nature** from afar, as if at the remote boundary of truth, seemed at first glance to offer another dim and uncertain light through the clouds, until, as the brightness grew stronger, the thickness of the clouds was overcome and revealed a more certain light. Bathed in this light, the mind became clearer and more confident in unearthing the secrets of nature. 

The first errors of the ancients—which are both the worst and the roots of all others—presented themselves to me concerning the principles of Nature. As I thought more deeply about **prime matter** <term>materia prima: in Aristotelian and alchemical thought, the fundamental, formless substance that has the potential to become any physical thing</term> and **universal form** <term>forma vniuersalis: the essence or organizing principle that joins with matter to create a specific being or object</term>, from which all other things flowed; and about the number of **Elements**, their qualities, their opposing positions, and their reciprocal actions, other things [seemed] to me from the real... <note>The sentence continues onto the next page.</note>

<sig>A iij</sig>

<vocab>prime matter, universal form, Elements, Nature, Philosophy</vocab>

<summary>Jean d'Espagnet describes his intellectual shift away from traditional Aristotelian philosophy, claiming that a "clearer light" of Nature led him to question ancient errors regarding matter, form, and the elements. He frames this as a process of critical re-examination (the "anvil") that leads to a deeper understanding of nature's secrets.</summary>
<keywords>Natural Philosophy, Prime Matter, Universal Form, Elements, Aristotelianism, Scientific Inquiry, Alchemical Theory</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's preface. The sentence from page 5 concludes here, shifting from the author's initial doubts to his total devotion to the "light of Nature."</meta>

...viewed from a more realistic perspective. <note>original: "conceptâ sententiâ"; completing the thought from the previous page: "other things [seemed] to me from the real perspective that was accepted."</note> Neither the authority of the more serious **philosophers**, nor their arguments—which were as ambiguous as they were clever—could turn my mind away from the light of **Nature**, to which it had entirely surrendered itself. 

Therefore, wonder drew my spirit into a love of **knowledge** <term>scientia: in this period, "science" referred to any certain, systematic knowledge of the world, often blending what we now call philosophy and physics</term>. This Love, which uses fiery rays as its weapons, snatched my kindled mind into the more sacred inner chambers of **Nature**. 

I hesitated for a long time whether I should share the **secrets** <term>arcana: hidden or mysterious truths of nature, often sought by alchemists and natural philosophers</term> drawn from that place with you, the students of **philosophy**. For I suspected it might be unwelcome to you, and for me, a task full of "dangerous dice-rolls" <note>original: "periculosæ plenum opus aleæ"; a classical idiom used to describe a project where the outcome is uncertain and the risks of failure or social backlash are high.</note>. Indeed, I felt myself warned by the passing years—which are the best of counselors—to learn wisdom from the misfortunes of others. For it was clear enough to me how many writers had suffered a "shipwreck of their reputation"; how stingy people are in offering praise, yet how quick they are to condemn the **labors** <term>lucubrationibus: literally "work done by lamplight," referring to the intense, late-night study required to produce a scholarly book</term> of others, and how bold...

<summary>The author describes how his devotion to the "light of Nature" led him to discover hidden truths, though he expresses deep anxiety about the risks of publishing these findings and facing the harsh judgment of his peers.</summary>
<keywords>Nature, philosophy, secrets of nature, scholarly risk, reputation, light of nature, scientia</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's preface, completing the thought from page 6 regarding the difficulty of challenging established intellectual traditions. The author moves from describing the risks of publication to a direct appeal to the reader's objectivity.</meta>

...how bold they are in fashioning and nurturing **masks** <term>laruis: literally "ghosts" or "masks," referring here to deceptive appearances or false theories that disguise the truth</term>, and how stubborn their minds can be in pushing away the **truth**. I realized, finally, how difficult and even dangerous it often is to tear out the deep-seated roots of accepted opinions and plant new ones in their place. 

At last, my love for the truth and for you—the fair-minded defenders of the **Light of Physics** <term>Physicæ lucis: in this period, "physics" referred to the broad study of the natural world and the laws governing it</term>—has triumphed. Thus, the same person who was the author of the search for truth has now become the one to publish it. 

Let me ask only one thing of you, so that you may sit as more impartial judges: shake off the great names of **Plato**, **Aristotle**, and the titles of the leading **Philosophers**. Strip away the authority of these great men; keep your own judgment independent, rather than remaining bound to them. As you unroll their great monuments <note>original: "monumentis"; referring to their enduring written works</note>, add a word of caution <note>original: "Præfiscini"; a classical Latin term meaning "may there be no ill will" or "no offense intended." The author uses it to show respect for the ancients while warning the reader not to be blinded by their fame.</note>, lest the popular charm of a famous name creep into your minds. 

Far be it from me to suggest that even a tiny bit be taken away from the fame or the greatness of those geniuses, whom I believe should be honored like **deities**...

<summary>The author describes the difficulty of uprooting old ideas and asks the reader to set aside the overwhelming authority of ancient philosophers like Plato and Aristotle to judge his work fairly.</summary>
<keywords>Plato, Aristotle, intellectual authority, natural philosophy, scientific truth, skepticism, tradition</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's praise of the ancient philosophers, transitioning from their individual greatness to the collective progress of knowledge over time. The author argues that while the ancients laid the foundation, the "sciences" are a work in progress that each generation must refine.</meta>

...they have always been; I consider every accumulation of praise and glory to be unequal to the glory of such great philosophers. They themselves took up **Philosophy** <term>Philosophiam: at this time, "Philosophy" encompassed all theoretical and natural science, including physics and metaphysics</term> while it was still wailing in its cradle, and they increased its strength so far beyond its infancy that those sublime souls left behind for posterity scarcely any hope of striving further. 

But the first age of a nascent and not-yet-matured **Philosophy** did not allow—even by the judgment of those masters themselves—for all the folds and meanders <note>original: "sinus & mæandros"; a metaphor comparing the complexities of the natural world to the winding curves of a river or the hidden pockets of a garment</note> of **Nature** to be fully explained, nor for her most hidden secrets to be perfectly and unerringly revealed. The fertile talents <term>ingenia: the natural capacity, wit, or creative genius of a person</term> of their successors have advanced these discoveries, brought to light what was hidden, refined what was rough, and provided support for what was confused. 

In this way, through the centuries, the **sciences** <note>original: "scientiæ"; referring to systematic bodies of knowledge</note> have been carried forward into a mature age; thus a long series of years has added a summit to them, as far as has been permitted by the powers of human intellect until now. Perhaps there are more things still to be sought than are currently possessed. Let it <note>referring to Philosophy or human knowledge</note> not be worn down by old age, so that...

<summary>The author argues that while ancient philosophers established the foundations of knowledge, the "sciences" naturally evolve and improve as subsequent generations refine discoveries and uncover Nature's remaining secrets.</summary>
<keywords>Philosophy, Nature, scientific progress, human intellect, ancient vs modern, discovery</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s defense of progress in knowledge. Having established that the "sciences" mature over time, the author now addresses the critics who view any departure from ancient "boundaries" as a betrayal of tradition, arguing instead that expanding these limits actually honors and strengthens Philosophy.</meta>

...garment, but a stronger **philosophy**; those who seek to prove their faith in a matter solely by the passing of years <note>original: "erroribus anni"; likely referring to the fallacy that something is true simply because it is old</note> are the very same who take that faith away. Do not condemn the innocent with a hasty judgment and an insufficiently examined case. If it seems a crime to have moved the sacred boundaries of **Philosophy** <term>Philosophiæ: the study of the fundamental nature of knowledge, reality, and existence</term>, do not flare up in anger or immediately pronounce me a sacrilegious man. 

Instead, consider this: have these boundaries not been advanced rather than removed? Have the rights of **Philosophy** not been restored rather than corrupted? Has her Majesty not been increased rather than diminished? 

From this, perhaps **Philosophy** herself will repay the favor; she will deny neither her patronage against the deceptions of the **Sophists** <term>Sophistarum: teachers or philosophers who use clever but fallacious and deceptive arguments</term> nor her protection against the ferocity of envy and ignorance. Like the barking of wild beasts—the former <note>referring to Envy</note> wasting away at the success of others, the latter <note>referring to Ignorance</note> driven mad by her own blindness—both petulantly break into the cultivated gardens of the **sciences** <note>original: "scientiarum"</note> and the pleasant fields of **Philosophy**, and those who should be praised...

<summary>The author defends the expansion of philosophical boundaries, arguing that modern advancements restore and increase the "Majesty" of knowledge rather than destroying it. He warns against "Envy" and "Ignorance," personified as wild beasts that threaten the cultivated fields of human learning.</summary>

<keywords>Philosophy, Sophists, advancement of knowledge, defense of science, Envy and Ignorance, intellectual boundaries</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page concludes the author’s introductory remarks. He continues the metaphor from the previous page where Envy and Ignorance were described as wild beasts attacking the "gardens of the sciences." This page completes the sentence and the preface, transitioning from a defense against critics to a humble dedication of his work to the readers.</meta>

...waste the labors of more gifted minds <term>ingeniorum: refers to the natural talent or intellectual capacity of individuals</term>. In vain will they <note>referring back to Envy and Ignorance</note> hurl their terrors <term>terriculamenta: items or threats intended to cause fear; "scarecrows" or bugbears</term> against me; I shall hold their efforts in contempt, so long as the divine power of **Truth** <term>veritatis: the Latin concept of 'Veritas,' often personified as a goddess of truth and sincerity</term> remains in my sight, under whose protection I shall walk safely. 

These offerings <term>libamina: originally liquid offerings poured to a deity, here meaning the first-fruits or initial products of his intellectual labor</term> of my leisure, accept with the same spirit in which they are offered to you; if these should please you little, or if you hold other things to be more important, graze upon this humble gift <term>munuſculum: a diminutive form of 'gift,' used here as a gesture of modesty</term> of ours with a light bite, as if caressing it. It will be not unwelcome for this very reason: that it has provoked you toward better things.

<summary>The author concludes his preface by declaring his reliance on the "divine power of Truth" to protect him from critics (Envy and Ignorance) and offers his work as a humble gift intended to inspire further discovery.</summary>
<keywords>Truth, intellectual talent, defense of scholarship, modesty, leisure, dedication</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page marks the beginning of the formal treatise after the introductory preface. It establishes the metaphysical foundations of the author's "Restored Physics," starting with the nature of God and the concept of the "Archetype" of the world, moving from theology into natural philosophy.</meta>

<image-desc>A horizontal row of six decorative fleur-de-lis printer's ornaments, each separated by a small dot.</image-desc>

# HANDBOOK
## OF PHYSICS
### RESTORED

#### CANON
#### I.

<margin>God.</margin>
**God** is the eternal Being <term>Ens æternum: a philosophical term for a being that exists by its own nature, without beginning or end</term>, infinite unity, and the fundamental principle of all things; whose Essence is immeasurable light: His Power is omnipotence: His Will is perfect goodness: His mere command <term>Nutus: literally a "nod," signifying that God's simplest intent or gesture is an absolute and finished action</term> is a completed work. To one desiring more than this, there remain only wonder, silence, and the deepest abyss of glory. <note>The author suggests that God's true nature is ultimately beyond human description, leading to a state of "holy silence" or "a-pophasis."</note>

<margin>The World.</margin>
2. <margin>9. The **World**.</margin>
9. The **World** is like a work of craftsmanship, rotated into a sphere. Its parts are like the rings of a chain, binding themselves together in mutual embraces. **Nature** is situated in the middle like a deputy <note>Original: *vicaria*. This implies Nature acts as an agent or representative of the divine will.</note>, carrying out the changing turns of things. The **Craftsman** <note>Original: *Opifex*. Referring to God as the master artisan or architect of the universe.</note> is present everywhere, like a busy blacksmith continually repairing his work.

10. Just as the totality of the **World** consists of a threefold Nature, so is it distinguished by three regions: namely, the **Super-celestial**, the **Celestial**, and the **Sub-celestial**. 

The **Super-celestial** region, which is called "intelligible" <term>Intelligibilis: things that are understood by the mind or intellect rather than perceived by the physical senses</term>, is the highest of all; it is entirely spiritual and immortal, and stands closest to the Divine Majesty. 

The **Celestial** region is located in the middle. Having obtained the lot of the most perfect bodies and abounding in spirits <note>In this context, "spirits" refers to vital, subtle forces or "pneuma" that animate the cosmos.</note>, it pours out innumerable powers and life-giving breaths through spiritual channels. It is incapable of decay <note>Original: *corruptionis impatiens*. In Aristotelian physics, the heavens were thought to be made of a fifth element (ether) that did not rot or change like earthly matter.</note>, yet it is not entirely without change, though such change only occurs once a full cycle of time is completed. 

Finally, there is the **Sub-celestial** region, which is commonly called the **Elemental** region... <note>The text ends mid-sentence; the catchword indicates it will continue with "nuncupata" (named/called) on the following page.</note>

**Vocabulary:**
**World**, **Nature**, **Super-celestial**, **Celestial**, **Sub-celestial**, **Elemental**, **Craftsman**

12. Just as it was permitted to the sole **Creator** of things to create whatever pleased Him out of nothing, so to Him alone belongs the right to turn created things back into nothing. <margin>Prime Matter.</margin>
13. The Philosophers believed in a certain <term>Prime Matter: the most basic "stuff" of the universe that has no form or characteristics of its own until it is shaped into an object</term> that is older than the Elements. However, because they knew so little of it, they explained it only slightly and as if through a cloud. They described it as being devoid of qualities and <term>accidents: properties like color, heat, or weight that can change without changing what a thing fundamentally is</term>, yet serving as their first subject. It is empty of quantity, yet it is that through which all things possess magnitude. It is simple, yet it is the seat of opposites <note>For example, it can host both heat and cold, though not at the same time.</note>. It is unknown to the senses, yet it is the basis of all sensible things. It is extended everywhere, yet perceived nowhere. It is always desiring forms, yet holding onto none of them permanently. It is the root of all bodies, which nevertheless can only be conceived by the operation of the mind and cannot be felt. Finally, it is nothing in act <note>original: "actu nihil." In Aristotelian philosophy, "act" or "actuality" refers to what a thing currently is, while "potentiality" refers to what it could become.</note>,

14. Aristotle was more cautious; although he believed the World to be eternal, he indicated a certain <term>Prime Matter: the most basic, formless "stuff" of the universe</term> that is first and universal. Avoiding its many intricacies, he discussed it in a sober and ambiguous way, saying it is better to establish* one and the same inseparable matter for all things—which nevertheless differs in its logical definition—from which the "first bodies" and all other things perceived by the senses are composed. He argued that this matter is their first principle and is inseparable from them*, but is always joined with <term>repugnancy: a philosophical term for qualities that conflict or cannot exist together, like hot and cold</term>; it is subject to <term>contraries: the basic opposing forces, such as heat, cold, dryness, and moisture</term>, and from it the Elements arise.

<page-num>24</page-num>

<sig>B</sig>

...as a continuum <note>The text begins mid-word, completing "continuum" from the previous page's discussion on the extensibility of matter.</note> so that it can be extended; for to flow is the property of moisture, and the continuity of every body is owed solely to the benefit of moisture; for moisture is the <term>glue: original "gluten"</term> and <term>solder: original "ferrumen"; the binding force that holds distinct particles together into a solid object</term> of the Elements and bodies. Fire, however, acting upon the moisture by heating it, makes it thin <note>original: "rare facit"; to rarefy or make less dense</note>; for heat is the instrument of fire, by whose aid it performs two opposite tasks with one and the same labor: for while it separates the moist nature from the earthy, it thins the former and condenses the latter. Thus, through the separation of heterogeneous things <note>Things of different kinds or natures</note>, the gathering of homogeneous things <note>Things of the same kind</note> occurs. By this <term>proto-chemical: original "protochymica"; referring to the divine alchemy used by God at the dawn of time</term> art, the uncreated Spirit, the artisan of the World, distinguished the confused natures of things.

**God created heaven and earth, etc.** <note>original: "Deus cælum & terram, &c."; a reference to Genesis 1:1</note>) this means that God, in the beginning of the **shaping of matter** <note>original: "informationis materiæ"; in the Aristotelian sense of "in-forming" or giving a specific shape and essence to raw material</note>, distinguished it into two highest principles: namely, into a **formal principle** and a **material principle**, which are heaven and earth. The words that follow indicate that by the name of "earth," one should understand that very dark mass of waters and the abyss, which had not yet been clothed in a specific form.

> (**And the earth was without form and void, and darkness was upon the face of the deep, etc.** <note>Genesis 1:2</note>) 

This mass the creator enclosed and bounded within the supreme heaven—namely, the **Empyrean** <term>Empyrean: the highest part of the heavens in ancient and medieval cosmology, believed to be composed of pure light or fire</term>—which is the first **formal principle** in Nature, though it remains remote from our immediate world.

<margin>23</margin> For when the **Spirit of God**, which is the splendor of divinity, was poured out at the very moment of creation upon the waters—that is, upon the moist and opaque face of the Abyss—**light** immediately appeared. In the blink of an eye, this light seized the more eminent and subtle part of matter and hedged it in with a lucid circumference, like a border or a rim, appearing much like a flash of lightning <note>original: "coruscatio"</note>.

# Restored Physics
<note>original: "Physicæ restitutæ"</note>

The source of the **Nature of Light**, the most excellent of all things that can be perceived by the senses, was rightly attributed to **Unity** <note>referring to the divine Oneness or God as the singular source of all</note>. From this source, these lower things <note>the material world below the heavens</note> might receive the breath of life. Hence **the Philosopher** <note>A common reference to Aristotle, whose works formed the basis of natural philosophy in the Middle Ages and Renaissance.</note> most excellently said: *The Sun and man generate man.* <note>original: "Sol & homo generant hominem." This famous Aristotelian phrase suggests that human life requires both the biological act of the parents and the vitalizing heat and celestial influence of the Sun.</note>

<margin>30.</margin>
It is not without probability that several philosophers have placed the **Soul of the World** <note>original: "Animam Mundi"; a concept of an animating spirit or vital force that permeates all of nature</note> in the Sun, and the Sun at the center of the **Universe**. For the logic of balance and Nature seems to require that the body of the Sun be distanced by an equal space from the source and origin of created light—namely, the **Empyrean Heaven** <note>the highest, most refined part of the heavens, often associated with fire and the dwelling place of the divine</note>—and from that dark center, the **Earth**. These two are the extremes of the entire Great Work <note>original: "opificij"; referring to the creation of the world as a masterpiece of craftsmanship</note>. Thus, the **torch of the world** <note>original: "fax Mundi," a poetic description of the Sun</note>, acting as a middle nature and a reconciler of both extremes, holds its seat in the center. From there, it may more conveniently receive the immense wealth of virtues from the highest pole and transmit them at an equal distance to the lowest depths.

<margin>31.</margin>
Before the drawing together <note>The Latin text "contradictio-" is a fragment, likely intended as "contractionem" or "gathering together," referring to the light being concentrated from its initial scattered state into the Sun.</note> of created light into the Solar body...

<margin>46.</margin> **Corruption** <term>Corruption: Latin *corruptio*; in this context, it refers to the breaking down of an existing thing so its components can be reused—decay as a precursor to new life</term> is far closer to the principle of **generation** <note>the process of birth or creation</note> than **Privation** is. This is because corruption is a movement that organizes matter through successive stages of alteration toward generation. **Privation**, however, does nothing; it carries out no work in the act of generation. By contrast, **corruption** promotes and prepares matter so that it becomes capable of receiving a new form. Acting as a sort of mediatrix, it exercises an enticement <note>original: "lenocinium"; often implying a seductive or persuasive influence</note> so that matter may more easily fulfill the desire <note>original: "libidinem"</note> of nature, and by its help, achieve a union <note>original: "copulam"</note> with a form.

Therefore, **corruption** is an instrumental and necessary cause of **generation**. **Privation**, on the other hand, is nothing more than the mere absence of an active and formal principle; it is like the "darkness upon the face of the Deep" <note>a reference to Genesis 1:2: "Et tenebræ erant super faciem abyssi"</note>—that is, it refers to matter that is unformed and dark.

# Restored Physics
<term>Physicæ restitutæ: "Restored Physics," a work by the 17th-century alchemist Jean d'Espagnet, aiming to reconcile ancient philosophy with natural observation.</term>

...purer and lighter, and more suited to motion and action.

<margin>57.</margin> Venerable **antiquity** imagined that the entire empire of the **world** was divided among the three brothers, the sons and co-heirs of **Saturn**, for the reason that it recognized only three natures of the **Elements**, or more truly, three parts of the **universe**. For by almighty **Jupiter**, who was allotted the dominion of the sky, armed with the three-pronged thunderbolt, and superior to his other brothers, those initiates of hidden mysteries <note>original: *arcanorum mystæ*; refers to the ancient philosophers or priests believed to have hidden scientific truths within mythological stories</note> understood the **ether**—the region of the celestial bodies—which claims for itself the right of sovereign rule over these lower realms. However, over the lower region of the sky, or our own **air**, they placed **Juno**, the wife of **Jupiter**, because this region is polluted with vapors, moist and cold, and in some way impure, and closest to the **female temperament** <note>In the gendered science of the time, "active" and "hot" qualities were considered masculine, while "passive," "moist," and "cold" qualities were labeled feminine</note>; and also because it is subject to the decrees of the higher realms, receives their influences, and communicates them to us...

For **Chaos**, the ancient parent of all things, was a certain subtle and dark **vapor**, or a moist substance of darkness, resembling thin smoke. # Physics Restored.

From this it happens that so many leaves fall dry at the first cold of **Autumn**; and the stalks of weak plants, as **Winter** presses on, are deprived of moisture and wither into extreme dryness. # Enchiridion

[Nature] does not allow [the elements] to destroy or overwhelm her own powers; but a more intense cold would, without a doubt, immediately conquer a weak and defenseless moisture <term>Humiditas: The fundamental liquid essence or moisture within all physical things.</term>, and by drying it out through powerful constriction, would exhaust it entirely or leave it invalid. In this way, if one of the **Elements** suffered ruin, it would necessarily follow that the works of the rest would be left imperfect, and the generation <note>The process of coming into being or biological creation.</note> of things would fail altogether. Therefore, to attribute the highest degree of coldness to **Water** is contrary to the law of **Nature**.

# Restored Physics

...every generation thereafter came forth from mud <term>Limus: Often referred to as "slime" or "clay" in alchemical and early natural philosophy; it represents the foundational fertile substance formed by the union of water and earth.</term>. In the generation of **animals**, mud is formed from the sperm and the menses, and from there, the animal arises. In the production of **vegetables**, seeds first pass through putrefaction <note>In early science, putrefaction was seen as a necessary stage of breakdown or fermentation that allowed a seed to "die" and release its life force into a new form.</note> into a subtle mud, and then they are solidified into a vegetable body. In the generation of **metals**, from **sulfur** and **mercury** perfectly mixed and dissolved into a fatty water <note>original: "aquam pinguem"; likely referring to a thick, oily, or unctuous liquid state in the metallic transformation process.</note>, mud emerges, from which metallic bodies are hardened through long decoction <note>A process of slow, sustained heating or "cooking" that matures a substance over time.</note>. In the philosophical dissolution of metals and the creation of the **Secret of the Philosophers** <term>Arcanum philosophorum: The "Philosophers' Arcanum," a hidden or profound medicinal or transformative substance sought by alchemists.</term>, mud is first drawn out from the purified and mixed seed of both parents <note>The "two parents" in alchemy typically refer to the masculine principle (Sulfur/Sun) and the feminine principle (Mercury/Moon).</note>.

<margin>70.</margin> **Water** is the basis and root of all moisture—indeed, it is moisture itself, from which everything moist receives its name. Therefore, **Water** can be rightly defined as the source and principle of the moist **Element** or of moisture <term>Humor: In this context, it refers to the liquid essence or vital fluid found within any physical substance.</term>, whose specific property is to moisten things with its liquid.

### Handbook

Those things are called **moist** which contain within themselves a watery moisture or liquid, to a greater or lesser degree. Moisture <term>Humor: In early natural philosophy, this refers to any fluid substance (not just bodily fluids). It was considered the "vessel" that held different qualities like heat or cold.</term> is capable of receiving all qualities: thus blood and yellow bile are humors imbued with their own heat, although they have their basis in the element of water.

**Strong water** <term>Aqua fortis: Nitric acid; a highly corrosive mineral acid used by alchemists to dissolve silver and other metals.</term> and similar liquids are powerful with a caustic and fiery virtue. **Burning water** <term>Aqua ardens: Literally "burning water," referring to distilled alcohol (ethanol), which was noted for the paradox of being a liquid that catches fire.</term> and many essences of things that are extracted under the form of oil or water abound in heat, even though Water—their root—is cold. This is because nature first imprints the various **signatures** <note>Signatures: The "Doctrine of Signatures" was the belief that the physical appearance or properties of a substance were "signs" imprinted by nature to indicate its inner power or medicinal use.</note> of her powers into the moist element, and infuses it from its very root with the principal qualities.

Moisture is the primary subject of Nature, upon which her first care and first labor are bestowed: in its liquid, she dilutes and mixes her manifold colors and indelible **tinctures** <note>Tinctures: Concentrated substances or "spirits" capable of transforming or coloring matter; in alchemy, the tincture represents the highest soul of a substance.</note>. To it, **spiritual gifts** <note>original: "spiritualia dona"; these are the invisible life-forces or astral influences that animate physical matter.</note> first communicate themselves; in moisture, they first take up residence and begin to act.

<summary>This page defines "moisture" as the primary medium through which Nature works, explaining how liquids like nitric acid and alcohol can possess "fiery" qualities despite their watery root. It establishes moisture as the first home of spiritual forces and the essential canvas for Nature's colors and tinctures.</summary>

# Physics Restored

<margin>71.</margin> The lower waters, divided in two ways, occupy distinct regions; for one part of them, being contiguous to the Earth, rests upon it as if on its own foundation and constitutes a single globe with it; the other part, flying upward, wanders through the region of the neighboring air with a shifting motion, and there, fashioning for itself masked images of bodies and manifold shapes of phantasms <note>original: "laruatas corporum imagines, multiplicésque phantasmatum figuras." The author is describing how clouds and mist take on the appearance of other objects—animals, mountains, or faces—as they drift.</note>, it drifts suspended to and fro.

<margin>72.</margin> At all times, a great part of the Waters dwells above, and traverses various tracts of air, driven here and there by the blowing of the winds: which was so established by the decree of Divine Wisdom from the day of creation, so that the open and free face of the Earth might arise for the **generation** <term>Generation: In early science, this refers to the process by which living things are born, grow, or are "brought forth" from the elements.</term> of things. For neither would the beds of the sea and rivers be sufficient to contain all the Water; indeed, if the whole volume were to rush down—the bolts of heaven being loosened—having overcome the level of the Earth, it would cover perhaps the highest... <note>The text ends mid-sentence at the bottom of the page, suggesting the next page continues the description of a global flood or the sheer volume of atmospheric water.</note>

<margin>73.</margin> **Water** is not raised into vapor by heat alone, nor does it condense into a cloud by cold alone. To both of these, the powers of the **Sun** and the **stars** contribute greatly—not only by multiplying the forces of the Elements, but also by a certain **Magnetic force** <term>Magnetic force: Here, the author refers to an attractive or influencing power exerted by celestial bodies, a precursor to the modern understanding of gravity and atmospheric tides.</term> that attracts or retains moisture to a greater or lesser degree, according to their diverse positions and the configuration of the heavens. From this, the differing character of the years and seasons is observed; for that mass of Waters is held in balance and restrained not only by cold or the density of the air, but by the powerful command of the celestial bodies.

<margin>74.</margin> So that divine justice would not lack its own punishments for vengeance, God made the **Ocean** that hangs over our heads volatile, and brought upon it the fiery bolts of **lightning**; so that those who [refuse] to be bent by love <note>The text breaks off here; "amore flecti" suggests the author is contrasting those moved by the love of God versus those who must be corrected by the fear of His storms.</note>

# Restored Physics
## Section E

...the audacity of men could not be restrained except by terror.

<margin>Air.</margin>
**75.** Those who attribute the highest degree of **humidity** to **Air**—on the grounds that it is with difficulty contained by its own boundary <note>original: *termino*; refers to the physical limit or shape of a substance.</note>, but easily by the boundary of another—are greatly mistaken. This is actually a property of **thin** and **liquid bodies**, not specifically of **humid** ones. Thus, such a property belongs more to **fire** and the **heavens**, whose natures are rarer <term>Rare: In early modern physics, "rare" (rarius) means the opposite of dense; a substance where the matter is spread thin, like a gas compared to a solid.</term>, than it does to **Water** or **Air**. For rare bodies, because they flow freely in every direction, cannot remain within their own boundary; for this reason, they require an external one. Only **dense** and **firm bodies** are held together by their own circuit and their own surface. Subtle bodies cannot do this because, due to their thinness, they liquefy and pour themselves out; and those that are rarer flow out more and possess less stability. From this, it follows that **Air** is a rarer body, but not a more humid one.

**76.** **Air** possesses no intense quality of its own...

<margin>77.</margin>

## The Physics of Elements

<margin>78.</margin>
The **lower region of the air** is similar to the neck and the upper part of an **alembic** <term>Alembic: A piece of distilling apparatus consisting of two vessels connected by a tube, used for purifying liquids through evaporation and condensation.</term>; for through it, rising vapors are carried to the summit and condensed by the cold, and soon, having been turned back into water, they fall back down by their own weight. Thus Nature, through diligent distillations, by **sublimating** and **cohobating** <term>Cohobation: The repeated redistillation of a liquid from the same matter to increase its purity or "virtue."</term> the water, rectifies and enriches it. In these kinds of operations of Nature, the **Earth** is the **cucurbit** <term>Cucurbit: The lower, flask-like vessel of a distilling apparatus that contains the liquid to be boiled.</term> and the receiving vessel. Therefore, this region of the air closest to us—bounded by the region of the clouds as if by its own vaulted roof—is denser and more impure than the other higher regions.

<margin>79.</margin>

<margin>80.</margin>

81. The leaders of ancient philosophy established **Fire** as the fourth Element of Nature in the highest region of the air, as if in its own sphere; this seemed so to them for the sake of symmetry and by suspicion, rather than by the light of truth. For no one should imagine for themselves a Fire of Nature other than **celestial light**; for that reason the **sacred philosopher** <note>Referring to Moses, the traditional author of the Book of Genesis.</note> in his **Genesis** <term>Genesis: The first book of the Hebrew Bible, which provides the account of the world's creation.</term> makes no mention of fire, because he had said that light, which is the true fire of nature, was created on the first day. Nor would he have omitted fire as a principle of Nature, since he made mention of the Earth, Water, and the birds of Heaven.

# Enchiridion

82. No one should imagine for themselves a region of burning fire touching the **Lunar sphere** <note>The Moon; in the old model of the universe, fire was thought to exist in a layer just below the moon's orbit.</note>, unless in a dream. 83. Such a devastator of Nature is welcomed as an element of Nature by neither the high air nor the deep earth. Instead, fire exercises a tyranny over the kingdom of Nature, whether it is ignited in the heights of the air, in the depths of the earth, or even on its surface. Thus, **Lullius** <note>original: *Lullius*; referring to Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316), a Majorcan philosopher and mystic known for his works on logic and alchemy.</note>, a man of lofty genius, rightly counts fire among the giants and tyrants of the World <margin>* chapter 11, First Testament <note>Refers to Llull's *Testamentum*, a famous work of medieval alchemy.</note></margin>. It is also correctly called "against nature," because whatever destroys Nature is opposed to Nature.

—fire <note>The text completes the word *Ignis* (Fire) from the previous page.</note> implanted both in the **Elements** and in mixed bodies flows down, having earned the name of **Fire**. From this Fire, even the sensible elements—the very provinces of our World—are formed and, as it were, brought to life. Yet, this fire clings more tightly when compressed within the womb of the earth, where the earth's density and coldness trigger an **antiperistasis** <term>antiperistasis: a philosophical concept where a quality (like heat) is intensified or concentrated when surrounded by its opposite (like cold).</term>.

<margin>89.</margin> That Fire of Nature, implanted in mixed bodies, has chosen the **radical moisture** <term>radical moisture: in pre-modern medicine, the essential fluid or "oil" that sustains the internal heat of a living being; once it is consumed, life ends.</term> as its own proper seat. <margin>92.</margin>
The Heat of Nature <note>original: *Calor Naturæ*</note> and the Light of Nature <note>original: *Lumen Naturæ*</note> are in reality the same thing; for they flow perpetually and uniformly from the same source—namely, the Sun—but they are distinguished by their function. Indeed, the duty of heat is to penetrate into the inner workings of nature, while it is the role of light to reveal the exterior. It belongs to heat to set the hidden natures of things in motion; to light, to bring only the "sensible accidents" <note>original: *accidentia sensibilia*; in the philosophy of the time, "accidents" were observable qualities like color or texture that could change without changing the essential nature of the object.</note> before the eyes. The rays of the Sun perform both tasks. Thus, the Sun is the primary instrument <note>original: *organum*</note> of nature; by its approach and retreat <note>referring to the seasonal and daily cycles of the sun's movement.</note>, all the operations of nature are variously governed, intensified, and relaxed through light and heat.

<margin>93.</margin>
The Second Universal Agent <note>original: *Agens universale*; a primary force or cause that acts upon the entire world.</note> is that very same light—not, however, flowing directly from its source, but reflected from the dense bodies it illuminates, such as the celestial spheres and even the Earth <note>original: *Terra*</note> itself. For by striking those bodies, the Solar light [stirs] their dispositions and faculties... <note>The text ends mid-sentence, suggesting it continues on the following page.</note>

...it; thereafter, it exercised the **Prefecture** <term>Prefecture: From the Latin *praefectura*, meaning a position of command or administrative oversight; here it refers to Love's "management" of the physical world.</term> in the generation of things.

**95.** The Author of Nature established the first bond of **Love** in natural things between **prime matter** <term>Prime matter (materia prima): In Aristotelian and Hermetic philosophy, the most basic, formless "stuff" of the universe that exists before it is given a specific shape or identity.</term> and **universal form**; between **Heaven and Earth**, **Light and Darkness**, **Porus and Penia** <note>original: "Porus & Penia"; These are personifications from Plato’s *Symposium* representing Plenty and Poverty, who were said to be the parents of Love (Eros).</note>—that is, abundance and want—and between the beautiful and the deformed. 

The second degree of love flowed from that first union—as if from a parental embrace—into the **Elements**. These elements, bound together in a brotherly spirit, divided the universal law of Nature among themselves in the most perfect proportion. 

The third and final degree is fulfilled in **composite bodies** <term>Mixtis: "Mixed" things; in the science of the time, this referred to all physical objects (like plants, animals, and minerals) created by the blending of the four elements.</term>. These, driven by the sparks of love implanted and innate from Nature, are moved toward the propagation and multiplication of their own kind. 

Divine Love established this triple bond of love as a **magic knot** in all created things, so that it might extend itself, like branching vine-shoots <note>original: *quasi per traduces*; a "tradux" is a vine-layer or shoot used for propagation. This metaphor suggests that Love spreads through the universe like a growing plant, connecting every branch to the original root.</note>, into each and every part of its handiwork.

<summary>This page describes Love as a "magic knot" that binds the universe through three levels: the union of primary opposites, the harmony of the four elements, and the reproductive drive in living things.</summary>

> *Cold things fought with hot, and moist things with dry.*

<margin>The qualities of the **Elements** are remiss <note>"Remiss" refers to a quality that is weakened or diluted, usually by being mixed with its opposite. For example, lukewarm water has "remiss" heat compared to boiling water.</note>.</margin>
104. Therefore, let no one imagine that **Nature** would admit "intense" fire into the family of her **Elements**; for such fire would be destructive, not generative. It would not be according to **Nature**, but against her, for she flees from violent things and delights in those that are tempered, from which all strife and opposition are absent. **Nature's** dominion does not endure the rage of burning heat or devastating cold, nor the imbalance of moisture or dryness; she rests kindly in temperance. Thus, let no one look for intense qualities within the **Elements** of things; they will instead find them to be "remiss," varying only by a matter of "more" or "less."

105. Therefore, whoever says that **hot** and **cold**, or **moist** and **dry**, are simply opposites, is mistaken. For the **Earth**, which was established by **Aristotle** <note>Aristotle (384–322 BCE) was the foundational authority for the theory that the physical world is made of four elements (Earth, Water, Air, Fire), each defined by two qualities.</note> as being primarily dry...

106. Those who establish the four **Elements** within as many **humors** <note>The "humors" were the four primary fluids of the body in ancient and medieval medicine: blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile. They were thought to determine a person's health and temperament.</note> acknowledge that a moist nature is capable of receiving the four elementary qualities—indeed, that it is their very subject. How then can they claim that those qualities are contrary to one another, when they place them within the same subject? For although those four **humors** are distinguished by their specific differences, they nevertheless have a single base common to them all and share the same root: namely, moisture. For yellow bile <note>original: "flava bilis." Also known as choler, associated with the element of Fire.</note>, which represents **Fire**, is no less a **humor** <term>humor|Specifically refers to a fluid or moisture that serves as a medium for life and qualities.</term> than phlegm, which they ascribe to **Water**. The same judgment applies to black bile <note>original: "atra bilis." Also known as melancholy, associated with the element of Earth.</note> and blood; they essentially blend the four **Elements** within moisture, doing so in a relative sense rather than an absolute one.

107. On the other hand, the **Particular Fire of Nature** is implanted and innate within every compound and individual thing; it flows from that universal fire like a stream and performs everything in its own **Microcosm** <note>The "small world"—referring here to an individual organism or human being which reflects the structure of the universe.</note> by analogy to what the Sun, its father, performs in the **Macrocosm** <note>The "great world"—the universe as a whole.</note>. 

As for our common fire <note>The author refers to ordinary flames, such as a hearth fire or a torch, which consumes fuel to survive.</note>, which opposes all generation <note>The process of creation, growth, and reproduction.</note>, living only on prey and subsisting on the ruin of others—a destroyer of life that destines all things to ash—who would not call it an enemy of Nature rather than a guest, or a source of destruction rather than a pillar of life? Furthermore, those fires that are generated in the region of the air <note>Likely referring to lightning, which the previous page categorized as an atmospheric "disease" or accidental hardening of vapors.</note> are due more to chance than to the deliberate plan of Nature.

<margin>*Earth and Water are not converted into one another by their position.*</margin>
Nor are the bodies of Earth and Water, though neighbors in their positioning, converted into one another; instead, on account of their proximity, they are merely mixed together. Thus, Water dilutes the Earth and Earth thickens the Water; from this, mud is created, which is neither body alone but a medium between both. If a resolution <note>original: *resolutio*. In early modern science, this refers to the separation of a compound into its original constituent parts, often through heat.</note> of this mud should occur by the force of heat, a complete separation of both natures will follow: as the Water flies away <note>The author uses *evolare*, describing the process of evaporation as the "flight" of the volatile element.</note>, the Earth settles at the bottom, and no mutual conversion of the two will take place. For the single quality they share—coldness—will not effect such a change, since the opposition of dryness and moisture is no less powerful at resisting change than the mutual agreement of coldness on both sides is at drawing them together. Furthermore, because the dryness and fixation <term>fixatio: the state of being "fixed" or resistant to the effects of fire and evaporation</term> of the earth are opposed to the moisture and volatility of water, only a single quality is found to agree to an alteration, while several others disagree and will prevail in their resistance. Nature itself will also intervene,

<margin>The first circle</margin>
**136.** **Sublimation** <term>sublimation: the process where a substance is heated until it turns into vapor and rises, often used in alchemy to purify a material</term> is the conversion of a moist and heavy nature into a light one, or an exhalation into vapor. Its purpose and usefulness are threefold:

First, so that through thinning <note>original: "attenuationem," the process of making a substance less dense, finer, or more refined</note>, a thick and impure body may be cleansed and gradually stripped of its dregs <note>original: "fecibus," referring to the solid waste or sediment left after a process</note>. 

Next, so that through sublimation, it may more abundantly acquire the virtues of the higher things <note>referring to celestial or spiritual influences from the heavens</note> which continually flow downward. 

Finally, so that through an evacuation of this kind, the **Earth** may be unburdened of superfluous and heavy moisture. This moisture, by occupying the Earth’s passages <note>original: "meatus," the internal pores or channels within the ground</note>, hinders the action of heat and the passage of natural spirits <term>natural spirits: subtle substances believed to carry life and movement through the world</term>; indeed, it even violently suffocates them. This removal of excess moisture takes away the cause of obstructions, relieves the Earth’s "nauseated stomach" <note>a metaphorical description of the Earth's inability to process elements when waterlogged</note>, and makes it more fit for **digestion** <term>digestion: in natural philosophy, the slow maturation or ripening of substances through steady heat</term>.

<summary>This page defines Sublimation as the first step in Nature's circulatory process, explaining how it purifies matter, attracts celestial influences, and prevents the Earth from being "suffocated" by excess moisture.</summary>
<keywords>sublimation, purification, celestial virtues, natural spirits, digestion of the earth, moisture, attenuation</keywords>

112

...the fire of Nature and the formal spirits <term>formal spirits: the organizing principles or "blueprints" believed to give specific shape and life to matter</term> flow into the seeds of things, in which all things necessary for generation are present, with the innate heat lying hidden within the moisture. <margin>\* Book 1, *On Diet*.</margin> Hippocrates very rightly affirmed that these two elements, **Fire** and **Water**, can do all things and that all things are contained within them; for from the first, two masculine qualities of heat and dryness flow out, and from the other, just as many feminine qualities of cold and moisture. In the generation of compound things <note>original: "mixtorum," referring to substances made of combined elements</note>, these qualities meet and are mixed together. Over these two primary Elements, as it were, the two greater luminaries preside: the **Sun**, the author of Nature’s Fire, and the **Moon**, the mistress of the humors <note>original: "humorum," the bodily or elemental fluids</note>.

**135.** <margin>The three wheels or circles of circulation.</margin> Nature fulfills the **Circulation** of the volatile Element through a triple action—as if using a specialized tool—namely: **Sublimation**, **Descent** (or Refusion), and **Decoction** <term>decoction: the process of heating or boiling a substance to refine it, extract its essence, or bring it to maturity</term>, which require different temperaments <note>original: "temperamento," referring to the specific balance of heat and moisture</note>. Thus, the well-ordered intention of Nature, wandering through various movements, [continues its] works...

# Physics Restored

...directs its interrupted activities toward the proposed end, and through opposite paths tends toward the same goal.

**136.** <margin>The first circle.</margin> **Sublimation** is the conversion of a moist and heavy nature into a light one, or an exhalation into vapor. Its purpose and utility are threefold: first, so that through thinning <note>original: "attenuationem," the process of making a substance less dense or more refined</note> the thick and impure body may be cleansed, and gradually stripped of its dregs <note>original: "fecibus," the sediment or waste matter left after a process</note>. Second, so that through sublimation it may more abundantly attain the virtues of the higher powers, which are continually flowing down <note>this refers to the Neoplatonic idea that celestial influences or "virtues" descend from the heavens into terrestrial matter</note>. Finally, so that through this kind of evacuation, the **Earth** may be unburdened of superfluous and oppressive moisture, which, by occupying its passages, hinders the action of heat and the passage of the natural spirits <term>natural spirits: in early physics, these were subtle fluids or gases responsible for life and movement within bodies</term>—indeed, it violently suffocates them. This withdrawal of unnecessary moisture removes the cause of obstructions and relieves the Earth’s sluggish stomach <note>original: "fastidientem... stomachum," a metaphor suggesting the Earth suffers from a kind of indigestion or lack of "appetite" for growth when waterlogged</note>, making it more fit for digestion <term>digestion: the process of slow, internal heating used to mature, ripen, or transform substances over time</term>.

**137.** Moreover, moisture is **sublimated** by the impulse of heat; for **Nature** employs fire as her own specific instrument to rarefy <note>original: "rarefaciat," the process of making a substance less dense or more airy</note> moist bodies. Therefore, vapors—from which clouds and rains are generated—are more frequently lifted up during the wintry and vernal seasons, because the **womb of the Earth** then abounds more in heat and moisture. Moisture is the **material cause** <term>material cause: the physical matter or "stuff" that makes up an object, according to Aristotelian philosophy</term>, while heat is the **efficient cause** <term>efficient cause: the force or agent that brings a change about</term> of vapors and exhalations. Nature exhibits a somewhat intense heat in the process of sublimation, provided it is restrained within the limits and breadth of a balanced **temperament** <note>original: "temperamenti," referring to a proper botanical or chemical balance where no single quality dominates destructively</note>.

<margin>
The
second
circle.
</margin>
**138.** The second lowering of **Nature’s wheel** in her circular work is the reduction of **spirituous vapor** <note>original: "spirituosi vaporis," a vapor filled with "spirit" or vital energy</note> into a dense and watery body. This is either the pouring back of moisture that has been rarefied and sublimated and then condensed again, or its descent into the earth so that it may drench the ground with its overflowing liquid, and soak it as if with a nectar-like and celestial drink.

<margin>139.</margin> **Nature** has three intentions in her watering: first, that she does not pour it out all at once, but gradually distills her overflowing moisture so that no flood <note>original: "gurges," implying a whirlpool or an overwhelming abyss of water</note> is created, lest the abundance of water block the path of the **life-giving spirit** <note>original: "spiritui viuifico," the vital energy believed by early modern scientists to flow through the earth to animate minerals and plants</note> passing through the bowels of the Earth, and extinguish the Earth’s internal heat. For that wise and just **governess** <note>Referring to Nature as a female personification of divine order</note> bestows all her benefits in **weight, number, and measure** <note>A reference to the biblical Book of Wisdom 11:20, a foundational concept for early scientists believing in a mathematically ordered universe</note>. Second, she intends to distribute moisture through various drippings and under different forms: namely, rain—sometimes denser, sometimes thinner—sometimes dew, and sometimes frost. By pouring it out now more generously and now more sparingly, she provides drink to the Earth, which is more or less thirsty according to its appetite or necessity. Third, she intends that these waterings occur not continuously, but by turns and mixed with other operations; for sunny days follow rains, and rains follow sunny days, just as days follow nights and nights follow days in constant alternation.

**Vocabulary:** Nature, irrigation, governess, by weight, number, and measure, drippings, rain, dew, frost, sunny days, showers.

<summary>This page describes Nature as a wise steward who distributes moisture to the Earth in controlled, varied, and alternating cycles to preserve the planet's internal vital heat.</summary>

140. Indeed, while immoderate heat dissipates these vapors and prevents them from condensing, intense cold binds and freezes them so tightly that they cannot release any falling moisture.

<margin>142.</margin> These three operations of Nature are so connected and correlated to one another that the end of one is the beginning of another; they follow one another in a necessary order according to Nature’s intention. 145. The fluidity of **Moist Nature** <note>original: "Humidæ Naturæ volubilitatem"; refers to the inherent instability and constant motion of the element of water</note> can be observed not only in the general harmony of the World, but also in the specific harmony of **mixed bodies** <term>mixed bodies: In early science, these are "mixta"—substances or organisms created by the blending of the four elements (earth, air, fire, water)</term>. For every animal, even a **little worm**, and every plant, even **Seaweed**, is a tiny World reflecting the image of the larger one.

<margin>153.</margin> One may affirm that the opinion of Democritus <note>Democritus (c. 460 – c. 370 BC) was a pre-Socratic philosopher famous for his "atomist" theory of the universe.</note>, that all bodies are made from **Atoms**, is not foreign to **Nature**; for reason and experience vindicate him from slander. Indeed, the ingenious **Philosopher** wished neither to veil the mixing of elements in obscure language, nor to keep it entirely secret. This mixing, in order to match the intention of **Nature**, must necessarily occur through the smallest and actually indivisible **corpuscles** <term>corpuscles: from the Latin "corpuscula" meaning "little bodies." This was the standard scientific term for atoms or particles before the modern atomic theory was finalized.</term>; otherwise, the elements would not coalesce into a continuous and natural body.

Experience teaches us in the artificial breaking down and building up of mixtures, which are performed through **distillations** <term>distillation: A process of heating a liquid to create vapor and then cooling the vapor to collect the purified substance. Here, it is used as an analogy for how nature separates and recombines matter.</term>, that a perfect mixing of two or more bodies does not happen except in a delicate vapor. But **Nature** makes her own mixtures far more refined and, in a sense, spiritual; it is more sensible to believe that Democritus understood these. For the thickness <note>original: "crassities," meaning the coarse density or "grossness" of physical matter that prevents it from blending perfectly.</note> of bodies stands in the way of their mixing,

# Restored Physics

<page-num>139</page-num>

...is set over by its own form, whose duty it is to rule over the organs and faculties of Nature, and finally over its own entire **World** <note>original: "Mundo suo." Thus, that which previously floated indistinctly and emptily in the vast ocean of universal Nature, is now called to a **Dominion** <note>original: "Imperium."</note> and sent forth.

<margin>176.</margin>
The formal act of **prime matter** <term>prime matter: (Latin: *materia prima*) the fundamental, formless "stuff" of the universe that has no qualities until a "form" is imposed upon it.</term>, as well as of the elements, informs nothing other than the principles of Nature themselves. This is the essential quality that makes a thing what it is—for example, the "form" of a tree that makes it different from a stone.</note> constitutes a perfect mixture; nor should one imagine multiple forms existing within it, since the elements themselves, by their mixing, take up only the task of shaping the body, not of providing its essential form.

<margin>177.</margin>

### Enchiridion

In the material world, things exist in **potentiality** <term>Potentiality: *potentia*; the capacity for something to exist before it is actually formed</term> before they emerge into the light; from there, they will flow in their own time and order, eventually emerging into **actuality** <term>Actuality: *actus*; the state of being fully realized or "real," as opposed to just being a possibility</term>. When they pass away, however, they flow back into the universal mass from which they had first come, just as rivers return to the sea. Every nature seeks out its own region, returning again and again to **Nature’s** workshop <note>original: *officinam*; literally a "factory" or "office," personifying Nature as a craftsperson</note> to be placed back upon her anvil for some new work. Perhaps this was the meaning of the **metempsychosis** <term>Metempsychosis: the "transmigration of souls" or reincarnation, a doctrine associated with Pythagoras</term> of the **Pythagoreans**, which was rejected long ago only because it was not understood.

<margin>183. Decay.</margin> When a compound body is dissolved, and falls into ruin through the defect of the **corruptible elements** <term>Corruptible elements: the physical building blocks of the world which, unlike the heavens, are subject to change and breakdown</term>, the **ethereal nature** <term>Ethereal nature: the higher, vital spirit or "breath" that gives life and form to physical matter</term> withdraws to its homeland. Then, a great disturbance and confusion of the elements occurs within the corpse, for they have lost their ruler <note>The "ruler" refers to the soul or vital principle that maintains the order of the body</note>. During this time, decay, death, and darkness reign within the desolate matter, until through that very decay the matter is made fit for a new **generation** <term>Generation: *generatio*; the process of being born or coming into existence</term>. According to the way the matter is then arranged, the power of heav— <note>The text cuts off mid-word; the catchword *cœ-* indicates the next page begins with a word like *cœlestis*, meaning "heavenly" or "celestial"</note>

<summary>This page explains the cyclical nature of matter, suggesting that physical things return to a universal source to be reshaped by Nature, much like metal on an anvil. It also describes "decay" not as an end, but as a necessary transition state that prepares matter for new life once the guiding spirit has departed.</summary>

...were watered by **ethereal milk** <note>original: *aethereo lacte*; a metaphor for celestial influence or energy</note>, its breasts would wither; a fact which the holy initiate of nature's mysteries <note>The "initiate" (mystes) here refers to Moses, traditionally considered the author of the Pentateuch</note> expressed well enough when blessing Joseph:
<margin>Deuteronomy chapter 33</margin>
*“From the blessing of the Lord is his land; from the fruits of heaven and the dew, and the deep that lies beneath; from the fruits produced by the **Sun** and **Moon**; from the peaks of the ancient mountains; from the fruits of the eternal hills, etc.”* In this mystical language, the Prophet promises the fertility of the earth through a generous **celestial influx** <note>original: *caelestium influxu*; the flow of power or vital spirit from the stars and planets to the terrestrial world</note> from the Sun and Moon.

191. How much that **spiritual food** contributes to the life of animals is something even a common person is forced to admit by the fact of frequent breathing, or the drawing in of external air. For Nature did not skillfully construct those **bellows** <term>Bellows: *folles*; a common early modern anatomical metaphor for the lungs</term> near the heart merely for the sake of cooling it—as is the common opinion of physicians—but also so that by their frequent ventilation they might breathe in the **ethereal aura** and offer up those **ethereal spirits** to the heart.

# Physics Restored
<page-num>153</page-num>
<header>Physics Restored</header>

...by whose breath and reception the **vital spirits** are repaired and multiplied.

<margin>192.</margin>
Philosophers call "spiritual natures" not only those which, being created apart from matter, are understood by the intellect alone—such as the **Intelligences** <note>original: *Intelligentiæ*; in medieval and Renaissance philosophy, these were the spiritual beings believed to guide the movements of the celestial spheres</note>, **Angels**, and **Demons** <note>original: *Dæmones*; in this context, refers to the Platonic sense of "daemons" or intermediary spirits, rather than purely malevolent entities</note>—but also those which, although they derive their origin from matter, nevertheless escape the examination of the senses due to their extreme thinness and nobility. Being very close to purely spiritual things, they are perceived more by reason than by sense. Natures of this kind are **pure air** or **ether**, the **influences** <note>original: *influentiæ*; the invisible flow of power from the stars to the Earth</note> of the celestial bodies, the **innate fire** <term>Innate fire: *ignis insitus*; the internal, essential heat that early modern thinkers believed sustained life and digestion</term>, and **seminal spirits**, **vegetative spirits**, **animal spirits**, and **vital spirits**, and other similar things, in which the nature of things itself consists more truly than in coarse bodies. Natures of this kind draw their lineage from **Heaven**, and by reason of their distinction from sensible things, they claim for themselves the name and right of "spirits."

<summary>This page defines "spirits" as both immaterial beings like angels and subtle, near-invisible physical substances like ether and vital heat. It argues that the true essence of nature is found in these "thin" spiritual substances rather than in heavy, visible matter.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual natures, Intelligences, Angels, ether, celestial influences, innate fire, vital spirits, animal spirits, seminal spirits</keywords>

# Enchiridion
<page-num>154</page-num>
<header>Enchiridion</header>

<margin>
*The Fire of Nature is spiritual.*
</margin>
193. It is appropriate to classify the **Fire of Nature** <term>Fire of Nature: *Ignis Naturae*; the internal, vital heat believed by early scientists to drive growth, digestion, and life itself</term> among spiritual things, since it cannot be perceived by any of the senses on its own; rather, it reveals itself through heat and other effects and accidents <note>original: *accidentia*; in Aristotelian philosophy, these are the non-essential properties of a thing (like color or temperature) that can change without the thing losing its core identity</note> in bodies. This is evident in animals, into which the imperceptible Fire of Nature pours a heat that can be felt; yet when that Fire and life slip away together, the **Elemental body** or corpse remains whole and unharmed, even though the mixture <term>mixture: *mixtum*; a compound body formed by the blending of the four elements</term> has been dissolved. In plants, however, because that Fire is weaker, it escapes notice and does not allow itself to be felt through heat.

<margin>
*Common Fire is among spiritual things.*
</margin>
194. Reason further proves that **Common Fire** <note>ordinary fire used for warmth or cooking, as opposed to the "Fire of Nature" described above</note> also belongs to the category of spiritual things rather than physical ones. For if it were corporeal, it would possess its own inseparable body, no less than earth, water, air, and other sensible natures, which consist of and are defined by their own bodies, and exist in and through them, [wielding] their powers...

<summary>This page argues that both the vital heat within living organisms (Fire of Nature) and ordinary fire (Common Fire) are "spiritual" or subtle substances because they lack a permanent physical body and are primarily known through their effects.</summary>
<keywords>Fire of Nature, Common Fire, spiritual substances, vital heat, elemental body, mixture, Aristotelian physics, natural philosophy</keywords>
